This is an alternative to #57457.\n\nCloses #59440.\n\n\"Adopting\" a GPU copy means a host-prefix hit points its page at a still-valid GPU copy of that host page (a GPU-side prefix-cache hit) instead of reading the page back from host.\n\nA host-prefix hit re-pins free GPU copies of its pages so the prefix comes back GPU-resident. Admission counts those copies as free blocks, but adoption pinned them before the hit's own allocation (hot region and new pages), which could then run the pool dry.\n\n#57457 fixes this by charging the pins at admission. That mixes host and GPU accounting in `HiSparseSourceManager.get_num_blocks_to_allocate`, and under pressure it defers a request that could run by reading its prefix from host, just to keep an optimization.\n\n## Changes\n\nHiSparse-only (`vllm/v1/hisparse/coordinator.py`):\n\n- **Adopt after allocation.** `commit_computed_blocks` records the pages to adopt, and `update_residency` (in `cache_blocks`, after `allocate_new_blocks`) adopts the copies that survived. The allocation always gets what admission counted, so no admission accounting is needed. A copy the allocation reuses is dropped by its reuse watcher and never adopted.\n- **Tail first.** HiSparse releases a prefix's pages tail first, matching `free_blocks`' convention, so pressure trims a prefix from its end.\n\nReplaying #57457's scenario (a resumed 3-page host prefix hit, `free_blocks` left in the pool):\n\n| `free_blocks` | main | #57457 | this PR |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| 5–6 | rejected | rejected | rejected |\n| 7–8 | crash | rejected | admitted, 0/3 copies |\n| 9 | crash | rejected | admitted, 1/3 |\n| 10 | 3/3 | 3/3 | 2/3 |\n| 11+ | 3/3 | 3/3 | 3/3 |\n\nNear the boundary this keeps fewer copies than #57457, because blocks freed after the copies are queued behind them. #59283, stacked on this PR, is an optional core change that restores 3/3 at 10.\n\n## Test Plan\n\n- `test_hisparse_prefix_hit_under_pressure_adopts_surviving_copies[7, 10]`: exact adopted pages, and no block shared between adopted copies and fresh allocations. `7` fails without the adoption timing, and `10` without tail-first release.\n- `tests/v1/core/test_prefix_caching.py` and `tests/v1/core/test_kv_cache_utils.py`, with `CpuPlatform` selected:\n\n```bash\npython cpu_pytest.py --confcutdir=tests/v1/core tests/v1/core/test_prefix_caching.py tests/v1/core/test_kv_cache_utils.py\n```\n\n## Test Result\n\n- Both new cases fail on main and pass with this PR.\n- CPU (`CpuPlatform`): 295 passed. 8 errors are `fixture 'caplog_vllm' not found`, from running outside the top-level conftest, and are identical on main.\n- H100, HiSparse unit tests across `tests/v1` (`-k hisparse`): 156 passed.\n- H100, `test_prefix_caching.py` + `test_kv_cache_utils.py`: 301 passed. "github:vllm-project/vllm:51574", "source": "github", "kind": "pr", "url": "https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51574", "title": "[V1 Scheduler] Fix Priority Queue Preemption Re-admission (#41951)", "body": "Fixes #41951 (supersedes #41952, #56843)\n\n### Why this change is needed\n\nIn v1 with `scheduling_policy=\"priority\"`, `PriorityRequestQueue` sorts waiting requests using `Request.__lt__`, which only compared `(priority, arrival_time)`. \n\nWhen a running request got preempted due to KV memory pressure, it was pushed back into the heap. If a fresh request with the same priority arrived earlier but hadn't started yet, the heap popped the fresh request first. This left the preempted request sitting idle while incoming requests overwrote its partially computed KV blocks, wasting GPU work and forcing a full recompute once re-admitted.\n\n### What changed\n\n1. **Sort preempted requests ahead of fresh ones (`vllm/v1/request.py`)**:\n   Added a `sort_key` property on `Request` and routed `__lt__` through it, boosting preempted requests with `-min(num_preemptions, 3)`. Capping at 3 gives re-admission priority without letting repeatedly preempted requests starve incoming queries.\n2. **Anti-thrashing in victim selection (`vllm/v1/core/sched/scheduler.py`)**:\n   Updated the preemption victim key to `(r.priority, -min(r.num_preemptions, 3), r.arrival_time)` so the scheduler victimizes least-preempted requests first instead of repeatedly thrashing the same request.\n3. Consolidated this fix with @Aravind0403 (who diagnosed the original issue in #41951) and incorporated their unit tests with co-authorship.\n\nThis only touches `priority` mode; default FCFS queues and speculative decode paths are unaffected.\n\n### Testing\n\n- `pytest tests/v1/test_request.py -k \"test_request_priority_comparison\"` (passes)\n- `pytest tests/v1/core/test_scheduler.py -k \"priority_queue or priority_scheduling_preemption\"` (10 passed)\n- `ruff check` and `ruff format` clean on modified files.\n\n### AI Usage Disclaimer\nThe architecture, diagnostic analysis, root-cause investigation, and design choices for this fix are entirely my own. false}, {"id": "github:vllm-project/vllm:53277", "source": "github", "kind": "issue", "url": "https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/53277", "title": "[Feature]: Cache-aware waiting QoS with optional cooperative prefill preemption", "body": "#### Summary\n\nAdd an opt-in V1 scheduling policy that improves time-to-first-token (TTFT) for cache-warm continuations under long-context contention:\n\n1. **Cache-aware waiting admission/order**: within a bounded window, prefer a waiting request when its *estimated remaining service cost* is materially lower because a local or external KV prefix is already available.\n2. **Optional cooperative prefill preemption (later phase)**: at an ordinary chunked-prefill scheduling boundary, checkpoint and pause a cold running prefill only when doing so is predicted to let a warm request reach its first streamed token significantly sooner, without losing the victim's completed work.\n\nThis issue is intended to track one concrete policy/use case. It is compatible with the scheduler extension framework proposed in #51608 rather than a duplicate of that framework.\n\n#### Motivating workload\n\nA single engine may serve many concurrent, long-lived agent sessions with distinct 100k–500k-token histories. Under load, one or more uncached chunked prefills can occupy the running set while a continuation with hundreds of thousands of tokens already present in local APC or an external KV cache remains waiting.\n\nFCFS is fair by arrival time, but it can produce poor interactive latency in this case: the warm continuation may need only a cache transfer, a short uncached suffix, and one decode step, while a cold request ahead of it still has a very large prefill remaining.\n\nThis is a QoS/tail-latency proposal, not a claim that cache affinity universally improves throughput. The corrected experiment reported in #42185 found no meaningful benefit for workloads sharing one already-resident prefix; multi-session workloads with distinct competing prefixes are the target case.\n\n#### Residency should be time-aware\n\nA large external-cache hit is not free: the cached prefix must still be loaded into GPU KV and resident while the request runs. However, that occupancy is temporal.\n\nFor a warm request, the approximate active residence is:\n\n```\ncache load + uncached tail prefill + actual output tokens / effective per-request decode throughput\n```\n\n`max_tokens / effective_decode_tokens_per_second` is a useful upper bound for the decode portion (not multiplication); under mixed prefill/decode load, effective per-request decode throughput may be lower. Output KV pages are allocated progressively, while the loaded prompt prefix remains resident until completion or preemption.\n\nThe admission objective should therefore consider **KV block-seconds**, not only instantaneous block count or cache-hit percentage. A huge warm prefix with a small output budget can still be an excellent latency trade: large compute avoidance, short expected residence.\n\n#### Phase 1: cache-aware waiting policy\n\nImplement an opt-in policy through #51608's future Score/QueueSort hooks, or initially as a narrowly scoped built-in scheduler policy:\n\n- Preserve explicit request priority as the outer ordering key.\n- Inspect only a bounded FCFS/priority-ordered candidate window.\n- Use side-effect-free, cycle-cached features.\n- Never perform connector RPC or blocking I/O in the scheduler hot path.\n- Consider an external hit only after the connector's asynchronous lookup has completed.\n- Keep deterministic tie-breaking.\n\nA candidate score should estimate TTFT improvement rather than rank raw hit tokens:\n\n```\nwarm_ttft_cost =\n    ready_cache_transfer_time(tier, bytes)\n  + uncached_tokens / observed_prefill_rate\n  + first_decode_step_cost\n\nresidency_cost =\n    required_prompt_blocks * predicted_active_seconds\n\nbenefit =\n    estimated_head_wait_avoided\n  - warm_ttft_cost\n  - admission/displacement cost\n  - weighted_residency_cost\n```\n\nUseful read-only features:\n\n- local cached tokens;\n- external cached tokens, lookup readiness, and cache tier;\n- estimated transfer bytes/time;\n- uncached prompt remainder;\n- blocks required for admission;\n- prompt-prefill versus active-decode state;\n- waiting age, explicit priority, and optional tenant/SLA weight;\n- output-token budget as a conservative residence estimate.\n\nThe KV connector API may need a side-effect-free snapshot such as:\n\n```python\nCacheAdmissionInfo(\n    ready: bool,\n    matched_tokens: int,\n    tier: CacheTier,\n    estimated_load_bytes: int,\n    estimated_load_seconds: float | None,\n)\n```\n\nThis API must not allocate/touch blocks, emit cache events, increment hit metrics, or start a transfer.\n\nFairness and anti-thrash controls:\n\n- bounded candidate window;\n- maximum FCFS-head wait / aging deadline;\n- minimum predicted benefit and hysteresis;\n- per-session/tenant bypass budget;\n- do not bypass preempted recovery work indefinitely;\n- expose decision reasons without high-cardinality metric labels.\n\n#### Phase 2: cooperative cached preemption\n\nQueue reordering alone cannot admit a warm request when running prefixes occupy the required GPU KV. A later opt-in phase could reclaim a running **prefill** at an existing scheduler-step boundary.\n\nRequired connector/scheduler contract:\n\n1. Select only a prefill victim; protect active decodes, remote-KV loads, and near-finished prefills.\n2. Identify the latest complete cache chunk common to every TP/PP rank.\n3. Ensure that prefix is durable and reloadable before freeing its GPU blocks:\n   - wait for store completion, or\n   - pin the blocks until all-rank completion;\n   - do not treat store submission as durability.\n4. Requeue the victim with an explicit checkpoint token count.\n5. Admit the warm request only if predicted benefit exceeds checkpoint/reload cost.\n6. Reload the victim later; recompute only an incomplete tail.\n7. On any connector timeout/failure, retain or safely recompute the victim rather than trusting partial external state.\n\nSuggested safeguards:\n\n- cooldown and maximum preemptions per request;\n- one cooperative preemption per scheduling cycle;\n- no preemption while connector store backlog/failure rate is elevated;\n- all-rank acknowledgement and failure injection tests;\n- no zero-progress preemption loops;\n- request cancellation and repeated-preemption coverage.\n\n#### Observability and evaluation\n\nSplit waiting/deferred reasons sufficiently to distinguish cache lookup, KV load, capacity, grammar, and other constraints.\n\nRecord:\n\n- candidate local/external hit and tier;\n- estimated versus actual load/prefill time;\n- scheduler decision reason;\n- prompt blocks and predicted/actual block-seconds;\n- checkpointed tokens and store-fence latency;\n- reload tokens/time and recomputed tail;\n- TTFT/time-to-first-stream won or lost;\n- preemption count, cooldown rejection, and fairness age.\n\nAcceptance criteria:\n\n- default FCFS and priority behavior is unchanged when disabled;\n- scheduler hot path performs no external I/O;\n- deterministic unit tests for bounded reorder, aging, explicit priority, and starvation;\n- connector tests for delayed/failed all-rank checkpoint and immediate block-ID reuse;\n- A/B traces with multiple distinct long-context sessions;\n- report sched", "created_at": "2026-08-21T14:48:14Z", "updated_at": "2026-09-30T07:07:04Z", "fetched_at": "2026-10-19T20:54:31Z", "state": "open", "merged_at": null, "parent": null, "ea21f7862229067bf4df0f8875971334e5ca2d9634062fc5beb2184146194571", "rank": -13.61251487252877, "excerpt_truncated": true}, {"id": "github:vllm-project/vllm:56774", "source": "github", "kind": "issue", "url": "https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/56774", "title": "[Bug]: Hidden-state extraction inflates KV cache memory requirements by 24× on a hybrid Mamba/MLA model", "body": "### 🐛 Describe the bug\n\nEnabling `extract_hidden_states` with Model Runner V2 on a Kimi-K3 hybrid Mamba/MLA model fails during KV cache initialization.\n\nThe hidden-state cache contains one logical layer, but each of its blocks is charged at the width of the largest target-model cache group: 24 layers. This increases its memory contribution from approximately **5.40 GiB to 129.61 GiB**, exceeding available memory.\n\nThis is a KV cache admission failure, rather than a CUDA allocation exception.\n\n### Environment\n\n- Model: Kimi-K3, hybrid Mamba/MLA\n- Runner: Model Runner V2 (`VLLM_USE_V2_MODEL_RUNNER=1`)\n- Local vLLM base: `41172e5e47`, with V2 extraction support ported from #49811\n- Tensor parallelism: 16\n- Extraction dtype: BF16\n- Hidden-state connector: custom Mooncake connector\n- `max_model_len`: 65536\n- `mamba_cache_mode`: `none`\n\nThe failure was reproduced on this local branch. It has not been reproduced against an unmodified current upstream checkout.\n\n### Error\n\n```text\nValueError: To serve at least one request with the model's max seq len\n(65536), (131.36 GiB KV cache is needed, which is larger than the\navailable KV cache memory (29.24 GiB).\n```\n\n### Diagnostic breakdown\n\nAll groups have a physical page size of **442368 bytes**.\n\n| Group | Spec | Layers | Tokens/block | Blocks needed | Admission contribution |\n|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| 0 | MambaSpec | 23 | 65536 | 2 | 0.0198 GiB |\n| 1 | MambaSpec | 23 | 65536 | 2 | 0.0198 GiB |\n| 2 | MambaSpec | 23 | 65536 | 2 | 0.0198 GiB |\n| 3 | MLAAttentionSpec | 24 | 384 | 171 | 1.6908 GiB |\n| 4 | HiddenStateCacheSpec | 1 | 5 | 13108 | **129.6079 GiB** |\n\nThe extraction cache stores six hidden-state vectors per token, each of size 7168:\n\n```text\nbytes_per_token = 6 × 7168 × 2 = 86016\ntokens_per_page = floor(442368 / 86016) = 5\npage_padding = 12288 bytes\n\nhidden-state pages alone:\n13108 × 442368 = 5.4003 GiB\n```\n\nHowever, the shared pool uses the maximum group width:\n\n```text\ngroup_size = 24\nrequired_bytes = group_size × page_size × sum(blocks_needed_per_group)\n```\n\nConsequently, the singleton extraction group pays for 23 unused layer slots on every block:\n\n```text\nhidden-state contribution = 24 × 5.4003 GiB = 129.6079 GiB\ntotal requirement = 131.3580 GiB\n```\n\n### Suspected cause\n\nIn the affected grouping path:\n\n1. `HiddenStateCacheSpec` is excluded from target-model grouping.\n2. The 69 Mamba layers are divided into three groups of 23.\n3. The 24 MLA layers form one group.\n4. The hidden-state cache is appended as a singleton group, with its page size aligned to the target groups.\n5. Both physical allocation and admission accounting use the largest group width.\n\nThe shared pool layout therefore introduces substantial unused capacity for the extraction group. Changing only the admission formula would not fix the physical allocation layout.\n\n### Local workaround and observed result\n\nWe modified the extraction grouping path to split target groups into singleton groups before appending the hidden-state group:\n\n```python\n# vllm/v1/core/kv_cache_utils.py\ndef get_kv_cache_groups(\n    vllm_config: VllmConfig, kv_cache_spec: dict[str, KVCacheSpec]\n) -> list[KVCacheGroupSpec]:\n    ...\n    if hidden_specs:\n        groups = [\n            replace(group, layer_names=[name])\n            for group in groups\n            for name in group.layer_names\n        ]\n    ...\n```\n\nThis preserves per-layer cache specs and uses the existing shared block pool with one-layer-wide blocks.\n\nObserved diagnostics after the change:\n\n```text\nextraction uses single-layer cache groups:\ntarget_groups=4->93 hidden_groups=1\n\nmax_model_len=65536\ngroups=94\nmax_group_layers=1\navailable_GiB=29.2441\nrequired_GiB=7.1480\n```\n\nThe engine successfully starts with extraction enabled.\n\nThis validates removal of the admission failure. Extracted hidden-state numerical equivalence and throughput impact have not yet been validated. Singleton grouping increases block-table and scheduler overhead, so it may not be the best general solution.\n\n### Additional comparison\n\nA run without extraction starts with a 2.3298 GiB requirement. However, it has 29 MLA layers and eight blocks per Mamba layer, compared with 24 MLA layers and two blocks in the extraction run. It is therefore not a controlled extraction-only A/B comparison.\n\n### Expected behavior\n\nAdding a singleton hidden-state cache should require storage proportional to its own data, without multiplying that storage by the number of layers in the widest target cache group.\n\nCould the grouping or allocation scheme accommodate this case without requiring one cache group per target layer—for example, through separate pools or a layout that avoids unused layer slots?\n\n### Related\n\n- #49811 — Model Runner V2 support for hidden-state extraction.\n\n### Before submitting a new issue...\n\n- [x] Make sure you already searched for relevant issues, and asked the chatbot living at the bottom right corner of the [documentation page](https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/), which can answer lots of frequently asked questions.", "created_at": "2026-09-14T07:25:19Z", "updated_at": "2026-10-09T07:29:04Z", "fetched_at": "2026-10-19T20:56:07Z", "state": "open", "merged_at": null, DeepSeek-V4.1, on top of the encoder side (#56227). The layers past the last KV-source layer (21-39 of 40 on V4.1-Flash) own only sliding-window KV, so in eager prefill steps they run on each request's last 128 tokens, cold prefills included. Simplified re-submission of #56752 (closed); no other open PR implements this.\n\n- `DeepseekV41ModelState.prepare_attn` prepares the kept rows as a sub-batch, the way a microbatch is prepared: one Triton kernel gathers rows, positions, slot mappings and the replay-layer KV start, and the sub-batch gets attention metadata and a forward context of its own. DP ranks agree on whether any rank trims and on every rank's replay token count.\n- `DecoderReplayLayers` gathers the layer inputs by those rows, runs the replay layers under that context and scatters the outputs back.\n- Only eager steps trim. A step that fits a CUDA graph (up to 1024 tokens by default) keeps the replay layers on its whole batch inside the graph: such steps are launch-bound, so the graph is worth more than the trim. `prepare_attn` already gets the step's graph mode, which DP ranks share, so there are no nested captures, no eager breaks and no runner changes; the diff is confined to `vllm/models/deepseek_v41/`, a config docstring and the CI job list.\n- With the fused MoE finalize of #58586, a decode step's cut layer hands its unfinalized `MoEOutput` to the replay layers unchanged, and layer 21's first boundary finalizes it. A trimming step holds a prefill longer than the window, more tokens than any step that defers its finalize, so the trim path only sees tensors.\n- Stays off, with a warning, when the PP stage does not hold every layer after the cut, under sequence-parallel MoE, PCP or microbatching, with an Engram layer after the cut, or with a drafter window wider than the target's. `--no-swa-bounded-replay` turns off both sides. A no-op on ROCm.\n\n## Test Plan\n\n```\npytest tests/models/test_deepseek_v41_replay_batch.py tests/models/test_deepseek_v41_decoder_replay_layers.py\n```\n\n- Accuracy: gsm8k 5-shot, n=1319, two passes against one server (pass 2 is all full-prompt prefix hits).\n- Performance: `vllm-bench`, random token-id prompts, prefix cache reset before every point.\n- Both on V4.1-Flash, TP4 and DP4 x TP1 x EP4 (DEP4), decoder side on vs off with the encoder side on in both (off via a local patch, not in this PR).\n\n## Test Result\n\nUnit tests cover the replay rows and sub-batch metadata (adaptive verification, prompt-logprobs requests, DP agreement), graph steps getting no replay batch, and the gather/scatter with the indexer buffers. pre-commit and mypy are clean.\n\n### Accuracy (GB200, V4.1-Flash)\n\n| arm | pass 1 (cold) | pass 2 (prefix hits) |\n|---|---|---|\n| TP4, decoder replay on | 0.9249 | 0.9234 |\n| TP4, decoder replay off | 0.9303 | 0.9249 |\n| DEP4, decoder replay on | 0.9333 | 0.9287 |\n| DEP4, decoder replay off | 0.9257 | 0.9181 |\n\n`exact_match/flexible-extract`; one standard error is 0.0072. 0 request errors. true}, {"id": "github:vllm-project/vllm:59923", "source": "github", "kind": "pr", "url": "https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59923", "title": "[Perf][DSv4.1] Exchange packed FP8 Engram rows across DP and feed wkv MXFP8", "body": "## Purpose\n\nDeepSeek-V4.1's Engram tables are already MXFP8 (FP8 rows with one ue8m0 scale per 32 values) and `wkv` is an MXFP8 GEMM, but the lookup dequantizes each row to BF16, DP replicas all-gather those 512-byte rows layer by layer, and `wkv` quantizes them right back. This PR keeps rows packed from the host table to `wkv`.\n\n- **Packed rows, direct MXFP8.** The lookup copies each row's 256 FP8 bytes and 8 scale bytes as they are, and one unpack kernel writes them in FlashInfer's MXFP8 activation layout (F8_128x4 scales). `wkv` takes them as a `QuantizedActivation`, so both the dequant and the requant are gone, and the output is bit-identical.\n- **One all-to-all per prefill step.** `EngramBatch` looks up every local Engram layer into one packed buffer and exchanges it with a single all-to-all, which sends each rank only its own tokens (264 instead of 512 bytes per head row, 1/DP of an all-gather), then unpacks all layers in one launch.\n- **Peer exchange for decode.** Below 128 tokens per rank, `EngramPeerExchange` replaces the hash all-gather and the per-layer row all-gathers: ranks publish ids in symmetric memory, look up their head shard for every peer's tokens, and read their own tokens back from the peers after a barrier. All layers share one lookup and one unpack launch.\n\nThe batched path serves DP-sharded tables (`embedding_across_dp` without DP-shared memory) with TP = 1, `head_dim % 128 == 0`, no LoRA (it wraps `wkv` after construction), and a `wkv` kernel that takes F8_128x4 scales (reusing `can_fuse_query_quant`). The peer exchange also needs SM100, CPU offload, and 2/4/8 fully connected GPUs; without it every slot uses the all-to-all, which on DEP4 HBM tables at 1–127 tokens per rank is 19–58 us faster than the BF16 path. Everything else, including TP, DP-shared tables, single replicas and ROCm, keeps the existing BF16 path unchanged. Communication stays on the current stream and `wkv` still runs in `Engram.forward`.\n\nAlso changed along the way:\n\n- `EngramBatch` is an `nn.Module`, so the peer exchange's pointer tables are model buffers that level-2 sleep restores.\n- `fused_q_kv_rmsnorm_quant` and the Engram unpack kernel share the F8_128x4 scale offset and size helpers.\n\n## Kernel microbenchmark\n\nThe benchmarks, E2E runs and the first accuracy table were measured on d593d7f5df. 3724967bb9 only drops paths that DEP + THP never takes and keeps its `wkv` outputs bitwise identical (see Test Result).\n\nEngram only, two layers (lookup, exchange, and anything needed to reach the MXFP8 `wkv` input), 4 x GB300 in one node. DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash table sizes (24 heads x 16M rows, 94.4 GiB per layer) as DP-shared `/dev/shm` tables or private THP shards, uniform random ids rotating 3 sets, 256 MiB L2 flush per sample, full CUDA graph replay, median of 40 samples of the slowest rank, best of 2 rounds. Since this PR's output is already the `wkv` input, the BF16 columns include their activation quantization (17 us for decode, 203 us at 32K tokens).\n\nDEP4:\n\n| tokens/rank | DP-shared (us) | THP + all-gather (us) | this PR (us) | vs THP + all-gather |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| 1 | 34 | 80 | 36 | 2.22x |\n| 8 | 34 | 81 | 38 | 2.13x |\n| 32 | 38 | 93 | 40 | 2.33x |\n| 64 | 44 | 106 | 43 | 2.47x |\n| 127 | 136 | 136 | 50 | 2.72x |\n| 128 | 140 | 136 | 72 | 1.89x |\n| 1024 | 2442 | 315 | 179 | 1.76x |\n| 4096 | 5326 | 1097 | 534 | 2.05x |\n| 8192 | 8224 | 2087 | 1010 | 2.07x |\n| 16384 | 11854 | 4047 | 1954 | 2.07x |\n| 32768 | 16008 | 7860 | 3800 | 2.07x |\n\nDEP2:\n\n| tokens/rank | DP-shared (us) | THP + all-gather (us) | this PR (us) | vs THP + all-gather |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| 1 | 34 | 73 | 38 | 1.92x |\n| 8 | 34 | 74 | 40 | 1.85x |\n| 32 | 35 | 81 | 43 | 1.88x |\n| 64 | 41 | 90 | 44 | 2.05x |\n| 127 | 66 | 107 | 49 | 2.18x |\n| 128 | 62 | 107 | 71 | 1.51x |\n| 1024 | 2015 | 228 | 168 | 1.36x |\n| 4096 | 4629 | 754 | 507 | 1.49x |\n| 8192 | 6873 | 1407 | 970 | 1.45x |\n| 16384 | 9511 | 2709 | 1879 | 1.44x |\n| 32768 | 12389 | 5268 | 3672 | 1.43x |\n\nDP-shared tables need no exchange, so at 1–64 tokens they are at most 8 us faster than this PR; from 1024 tokens this PR is 3.4–13.6x faster than them. "issue", "url": "https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/30696", "title": "[RFC]: Per-instance EPLB metrics", "body": "### 🚀 The feature, motivation and pitch\n\n(co-authored with @sallyom and Claude)\n\nIn replicated vLLM deployments behind a load balancer, the load balancer has no visibility into EPLB (Expert-Parallel Load Balancing) state when making routing decisions. EPLB dynamically replicates heavily-used experts across EP ranks to balance load, and instances experiencing high expert load imbalance or active rebalancing operations have degraded capacity. Without metrics exposing this state, load balancers cannot avoid routing traffic to struggling instances, leading to suboptimal performance and increased tail latencies.\n\nEPLB already tracks and logs this information to stdout when `--eplb-log-balancedness` is enabled:\n\n```\nEPLB step: 2500: avg_tokens=1024, max_tokens=2048, balancedness=0.5000\n```\n\nBut these are obviously not queryable by load balancers. This proposal exports the same data that's currently logged as Prometheus metrics, enabling intelligent load balancing decisions based on instance health.\n\nNote - these stats are EP-group-wide, and should not be reported per-engine (i.e. with the `engine_idx` label), instead they should probably only be recorded on DP rank 0.\n\n## Proposed Metrics\n\n| Metric Name | Type | Description\n|-------------|------|-------------|\n| `vllm:eplb_avg_tokens_per_rank` | Gauge | Average tokens per EP rank in current interval |\n| `vllm:eplb_max_tokens_per_rank` | Gauge | Maximum tokens across EP ranks in current interval |\n| `vllm:eplb_rebalancing` | Gauge | Whether EPLB is currently rebalancing (0 or 1) |\n| `vllm:eplb_rebalance_events_total` | Counter | Total number of EPLB rebalancing operations |\n\n## Sample PromQL\n\n```promql\n# Imbalance ratio - 0.5 means 50% balanced\n(vllm:eplb_avg_tokens_per_rank / vllm:eplb_max_tokens_per_rank)\n\n# Relative imbalance: avoid instances where one rank is 2x overloaded\n(vllm:eplb_max_tokens_per_rank / vllm:eplb_avg_tokens_per_rank) > 2.0\n\n# Absolute imbalance: avoid instances with severe token gap\n(vllm:eplb_max_tokens_per_rank - vllm:eplb_avg_tokens_per_rank) > 5000\n\n# Hybrid: high load AND poor balance (most dangerous)\nvllm:eplb_max_tokens_per_rank > 15000\nAND (vllm:eplb_max_tokens_per_rank / vllm:eplb_avg_tokens_per_rank) > 1.5\n\n# Alert on thrashing\nrate(vllm:eplb_rebalance_events_total[5m]) > 2\n\n# Reduce traffic to instance currently rebalancing\nvllm:eplb_rebalancing == 1\n```\n\n### Alternatives\n\n_No response_\n\n### Additional context\n\n_No response_\n\n### Before submitting a new issue...\n\n- [x] Make sure you already Both were independently reproduced while investigating #59623.\r\n\r\n- Retain the newest non-`None` load counts while draining client statistics messages, while applying wave/running state from the final message.\r\n- Publish overdue coordinator snapshots while input remains readable for independent engines or when a previous-step MoE snapshot is available; preserve the existing quiet-poll fallback otherwise.\r\n- Add a real-ZMQ client regression covering interleaved load/state updates, both engine orientations, final wave/running state, and routing to the engine with spare capacity.\r\n- Add coordinator regressions for repeated dense publication under continuous input, publication of an available previous-step MoE snapshot, and preservation of the MoE fallback.\r\n\r\nThese defects were found while investigating [PR #59623](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59623) and the [MI355 Distributed DP Basic failure in AMD Buildkite #13954](https://buildkite.com/vllm/amd-ci/builds/13954#01a0f6b2-7bd5-4356-8d4e-4b9e9e581895). That job completed all 100 requests with a 68/32 split and failed the test's distribution assertion; correcting that test remains scoped to #59623. Earlier [PR #47420](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/47420) improved tie-breaking, and [PR #49204](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/49204) added dense request-count publication and an exact local inflight floor. The new reproductions establish two remaining defects in load-stat delivery and consumption: the coordinator can postpone updates indefinitely under continuous input, and the client can discard a received snapshot before making its next routing decision. This PR fixes those independently reproduced defects; neither is established as the cause of the CI job's 68/32 split, and the change does not claim to resolve the historical multi-day imbalance reported in [issue #40279](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/40279).\r\n\r\nThe coordinator previously published only after a poll returned no events; the change also permits publication when the deadline has expired and a suitable snapshot is available. It is not an unconditional 100 ms guarantee: MoE still requires a previous-step snapshot or a quiet poll, and a deadline crossed during polling can require one more loop iteration. The client previously decoded only the final drained message, losing a preceding full snapshot when that message had `counts=None`; decoding each message preserves partial-update semantics without changing the message format or routing score. Dense coordination does not emit the wave-only messages involved in the client defect, so that defect cannot explain the supplied dense CI failure.\r\n\r\nAI assistance was used for the investigation, implementation, validation, and this draft.", "created_at": "2026-10-02T01:04:24Z", "updated_at": "2026-10-02T19:22:28Z", "fetched_at": "2026-10-19T20:54:58Z", "state": "closed", "merged_at": null, "parent": null, "tags": ["moe", "speculation"], "metadata": {"number": 59715, "labels": [{"id": 5135047172, "node_id": "LA_kwDOI7xefs8AAAABMhKaBA", "url": "https://api.github.com/repos/vllm-project/vllm/labels/bug", "name": "bug", "color": "d73a4a", "default": true, "description": "Something isn't working", "archived_at": null, "archived_by": null}], "comments": 4, "state_reason": null}, "content_hash": "9a7c96175676758a65445ae0dd9c273499c730a7813532b3b157af92f4988da9", "rank": -17.146359148186754, "excerpt_truncated": false}, {"id": "github:vllm-project/vllm:59168", "source": "github", "kind": "pr", "url": "https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59168", "title": "[EPLB] Add the MoE Load Balancer policy: pluggable expert placement and replica routing", "body": "## Purpose\n\nAdd an optional expert-parallel load-balancing backend to EPLB: `--eplb-config '{\"policy\": \"mlb\", ...}'` delegates expert **placement** (the EPLB rearrangement) and, optionally, **per-token replica routing** to the [MoE Load Balancer](https://github.com/xutizhou/moe_load_balancer) (`moe_load_balancer`), a framework-neutral library of load-balancing policies (DeepSeek EPLB hierarchical/global, LPLB, Waterfill for the shared expert, and UltraEP's online placement/quota refresh). vLLM keeps the mechanism, the library keeps the policies.\n\nWhat the PR adds, all inactive unless `policy: \"mlb\"` is selected (the package is imported lazily; "[CI/Build] Make DP load test aware of engine speed", "body": "## Overview\r\n\r\n`test_load` requires every engine to finish more than `1/(DP+1)` of the requests, which assumes the engines are equally fast and fails on a correct load-aware balancer when one of them is not. The test fix has to come first regardless: a balancer that sends even less work to a slower engine makes the current assertion fail more often, not less.\r\n\r\n## Claims\r\n\r\n- `test_load` no longer fails when one engine is measurably slower, which is correct behaviour for a load-aware balancer \r\n   rather than imbalance. Observed as a flake on a 2-GPU CI step.\r\n- A genuinely broken balancer is still caught: forcing 100% or 70% of the requests onto one engine on equal GPUs still \r\n   fails, at  `DP_SIZE=2` and `4`.\r\n- The bound for equal engines gets slightly stricter: at `DP_SIZE=2` from 34 to 40 of 100 requests; at `DP_SIZE=4` it lands \r\n   on 20, unchanged.\r\n\r\n## Validation\r\n\r\n```\r\nTP_SIZE=1 DP_SIZE=2 pytest -v -s tests/v1/distributed/test_async_llm_dp.py\r\n```\r\n\r\n`23 passed, 8 skipped`. In the failing run the engine speeds differed by 21x (median inter-token latency 1.575 ms vs 33.03 ms). The slow engine's capacity was about 4.5% of the requests and it received 30%, so the test failed in a run where the balancer had been *too* generous to it. A constant bound also cannot separate the two cases: a 70/30 split is correct on unequal engines and a real bug on equal ones.\r\n\r\nThe stats logger the test already installs now also collects `iteration_stats.inter_token_latencies_iter`. The assertion takes the median per engine; if the slowest is within 1.5x of the fastest it requires 80% of an even share, otherwise only that no engine went unused. Latency is used rather than throughput because throughput depends on how many requests the balancer sent,\r\nwhich would make the check circular — verified by a skewed 72/10/10/8 split where the measured per-token latencies stayed within 1.68-1.81 ms on all four engines. The failure message now carries the measured latencies and the comparability\r\nverdict.\r\n\r\nBoth constants come from the measurements above; healthy splits never left 46-54 band over 15 runs. All of those runs are from a single two-GPU and a single four-GPU machine, so the spread on other configurations in CI is not something I can measure locally — the CI run on this PR is what exercises them. CPU offloading can extend capacity within an engine, while an external KV store can preserve and reuse prefixes across engines, DP ranks, nodes, and engine restarts. The open [vLLM UMBP feature request](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/48191) tracks this need for AMD deployments.\n\nMORI-UMBP provides an AMD-focused storage path with HIP-visible GPU-buffer registration, NUMA-aware host DRAM, MORI-IO RDMA, SSD backends, tier-placement policy, and scheduler-visible KV location and hit metadata. Integrating it into vLLM provides several concrete benefits:\n\n- **Shareable capacity:** standalone UMBP places the node-local KV pool in a separate process so DP ranks and model instances can share DRAM. This also allows round-robin traffic to reuse KV across DP ranks.\n- **Direct GPU-to-store movement:** grouped ranged operations move between registered vLLM KV buffers and UMBP without requiring a connector-owned contiguous host staging copy.\n- **Cache lifetime independent of the engine:** standalone storage survives worker restart or rolling upgrade, avoiding a complete host-cache warm-up.\n- **Global placement and routing:** distributed UMBP can use cluster-wide capacity and location information instead of making every placement or eviction decision from one rank's local view.\n- **Replication-aware storage:** byte-identical TP replicas can share one object identity in shared storage, while rank-sharded data retains distinct shard identity. Writer election can additionally reduce duplicate traffic when every reader can reach the elected writer's object.\n- **Layer-wise overlap:** grouped ranged I/O, batched offload, and per-layer completion allow later-layer KV loading to overlap earlier-layer compute.\n- **Tiered capacity:** UMBP can extend the same object namespace from DRAM to SSD and optional UMBP-owned HBM without adding medium-specific branches to the vLLM connector.\n\nThe initial integration should not require a redesign of vLLM's connector API. `MooncakeStoreConnector` provides a useful structural precedent: one `KVConnectorBase_V1` implementation dispatches to scheduler-side lookup/planning and worker-side registration/transfer logic.\n\n`UMBPStoreConnector` follows that vLLM-facing structure while keeping UMBP semantics behind `IUMBPRuntime`. Embedded mode is worker-private and masterless; standalone mode shares a node-local service; distributed mode uses Master routing, peer-owned slots, MORI-IO, and heartbeat-acknowledged publication. Identity, layout, metadata, completion, preemption, and failure-to-recompute remain common across all three modes.\n\n## Proposed Change\n\n### Summary\n\nThis RFC proposes adding one vLLM V1 `UMBPStoreConnector` backed by MORI-UMBP. It follows the proven scheduler/worker organization of `MooncakeStoreConnector` while keeping UMBP-specific lookup, transfer, and publication semantics behind `IUMBPRuntime`.\n\nThe connector supports embedded, standalone, and distributed UMBP modes without duplicating vLLM-facing code. All modes share replication-aware object identity, KV layout, grouped/layer-wise transfer planning, metadata, job lifecycle, pinning, completion, and failure-to-recompute behavior; only runtime topology and data movement differ.\n\nImplementation proceeds through small PRs, beginning with the shared connector framework and embedded mode, then adding standalone and distributed runtimes. A broader backend-neutral connector for UMBP, Mooncake, and other stores is recorded as an open architectural discussion and does not block UMBP delivery.\n\n### Goals\n\n- Add one vLLM V1 `UMBPStoreConnector` following the existing `KVConnectorBase_V1` scheduler/worker lifecycle.\n- Support embedded, standalone, and distributed UMBP modes through one shared connector framework.\n- Select the UMBP runtime mode at connector initialization without changing scheduler/worker connector code.\n- Use vLLM content-derived block hashes, never physical GPU block IDs, as the basis of persistent object identity.\n- Make object identity replication-aware per KV-cache group:\n  - sharded TP data keeps a logical shard/rank component;\n  - byte-identical TP replicas use one shared object key when the selected runtime storage is shared by those ranks;\n  - private embedded pools retain the physical copy needed by each process, even if their logical key is identical.\n- Avoid redundant writes only when the runtime can prove that one writer's object is visible to every rank that will read it. Key deduplication and writer election are separate optimizations.\n- Keep KV layout planning, connector metadata, transfer job state, pinning, completion, and recomputation logic common across all modes.\n- Use UMBP ranged Put/Get operations for non-contiguous GPU KV regions.\n- Support both bulk and layer-wise execution through the shared framework: submit grouped asynchronous range operations, wait only for the layer about to execute, and batch completed-layer offloads.\n- Register vLLM GPU KV allocations once and pass registered GPU regions directly to UMBP/MORI-IO without connector-owned contiguous staging.\n- Treat missing, evicted, timed-out, stale, or partially transferred objects as cache misses and safely recompute them.\n- Keep UMBP transport, routing, and publication details out of vLLM scheduler, model-runner, and attention code.\n- Incubate through `kv_connector_module_path` against a pinned vLLM revision before considering upstream registration.\n\n### Architecture\n\n![vLLM and MORI-UMBP architecture](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/charxwu/vllm/b6827f6b9ad236a7806f1e60fe739494eb81a7ed/docs/assets/vllm-umbp-architecture-v2.svg)\n\n_Figure 1. `UMBPStoreConnector` bridges vLLM's scheduler and worker lifecycle to UMBP's control and data planes._\n\nvLLM owns request scheduling and GPU KV blocks. `UMBPStoreConnector` maps vLLM's scheduler/worker lifecycle onto UMBP control and data operations. UMBP owns routing, transfer, publication, and storage-tier policy.\n\n![Shared UMBP connector and three runtime modes](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/charxwu/vllm/b6827f6b9ad236a7806f1e60fe739494eb81a7ed/docs/assets/vllm-umbp-connector-v6.svg)\n\n_Figure 2. Shared connector code sits above `IUMBPRuntime`; embedded, standalone, and distributed implement only mode-specific operations._\n\nAll modes share connector identity, metadata, layout, pinning, completion, and failure behavior. Only the runtime implementation changes.\n\n`IUMBPRuntime` exposes lookup, direct GPU-buffer registration, grouped load, per-layer wait, batched store, publication, and shutdown operations. Runtime handles receive UMBP keys and transfer plans rather than vLLM request or attention objects.\n\nThe shared identity planner classifies each cache group as rank-sharded or rank-replicated. It retains shard identity for different bytes and collapses the TP key component for byte-identical replicas only when runtime visibility allows those ranks to share one stored object.", "created_at": "https://huggingface.co/papers/2604.08826", "title": "HiFloat4 Format for Language Model Pre-training on Ascend NPUs", "body": "Large foundation models have become central to modern machine learning, with performance scaling predictably with model size and data. However, training and deploying such models incur substantial computational and memory costs, motivating the development of low-precision training techniques. Recent work has demonstrated that 4-bit floating-point (FP4) formats--such as MXFP4 and NVFP4--can be successfully applied to linear GEMM operations in large language models (LLMs), achieving up to 4x improvements in compute throughput and memory efficiency compared to higher-precision baselines. In this work, we investigate the recently proposed HiFloat4 FP4 format for Huawei Ascend NPUs and systematically compare it with MXFP4 in large-scale training settings. All experiments are conducted on Ascend NPU clusters, with linear and expert GEMM operations performed entirely in FP4 precision. We evaluate both dense architectures (e.g., Pangu and LLaMA-style models) and mixture-of-experts (MoE) models, where both standard linear layers and expert-specific GEMMs operate in FP4. Furthermore, we explore stabilization techniques tailored to FP4 training that significantly reduce numerical degradation, maintaining relative error within 1% of full-precision baselines while preserving the efficiency benefits of 4-bit computation. On Hopper (SM90) vLLM runs these through the Marlin W4A16 MoE\nkernel. This adds an opt-in path (`VLLM_DSV4_FP4_DEQUANT=1`) that losslessly\nre-encodes the MXFP4 experts to block-FP8 (e4m3, 128×128 scales) at load time\nand runs them through the existing block-FP8 MoE kernel (FP8 tensor cores),\ngiving materially higher prefill throughput on Hopper. The trade-off is larger\nexpert weight memory (FP8 is 2× MXFP4), so it is strictly opt-in; MXFP4/Marlin\nremains the default.\n\nThe re-encode is **bit-exact**: MX scales are pure powers of two (e8m0) and\ne4m3 has enough mantissa/range to absorb the per-32-group scale ratio into the\nstored value (`max_fp4 6.0 × 2^6 = 384 < 448`), so no precision is lost relative\nto the MXFP4 weights.\n\n### Why Hopper specifically\n\nThe native MXFP4 W4A8/W4-MXFP8 MoE backends (`DEEPGEMM_MXFP4`,\n`FLASHINFER_TRTLLM_MXFP4_MXFP8`, `FLASHINFER_CUTLASS_MXFP4_MXFP8`) keep 4-bit\nweight storage *and* use FP8 tensor cores, but their `is_supported_config`\ngates them to Blackwell (`is_device_capability_family(100)`/`(120)`). On SM90\nonly Marlin W4A16 passes, so Hopper has no FP8-tensor-core MoE path for these\ncheckpoints today. A native SM90 W4AFP8 grouped MoE is still an open request\n(vllm-project/vllm#49977). This opt-in dequant→FP8 gives Hopper that fast path\nnow; the cost is 2× expert weight memory (FP8 vs MXFP4). A true SM90 W4A8\nkernel would supersede it by keeping 4-bit storage — left as follow-up.\n\n## Not a duplicate\n\nSearched open PRs/issues (`mxfp4 fp8 dequant experts`, `DeepSeek-V4 fp4 fp8`,\n`deepseek_v4 hopper`, `fp8 experts hopper block`). A checkpoint that quantizes some layers to NVFP4 and others to FP8 declares `quant_algo: MIXED_PRECISION`, so `ModelOptMixedPrecisionConfig.override_quantization_method` claims it and `self.quantization` becomes `modelopt_mixed`. The check returns False even though most of the model is NVFP4.\n\nThe consequence is not cosmetic. `enable_act_fusion()` (`vllm/config/vllm.py`) enables `pass_config.fuse_act_quant` for FP4 models precisely because, in its own words, *\"FP4 quant is always custom so Inductor cannot fuse it\"*. With NVFP4 undetected, `fuse_act_quant` stays False at **every** optimization level, `ActivationQuantFusionPass` never runs, and the fused CUDA op `silu_and_mul_nvfp4_quant` is unreachable for models without manual act+quant fusion (currently everything except Llama and Nemotron-H), since that pass is their only route to it. The model then runs SiLU-and-mul and `scaled_fp4_quant` as two kernels with a bf16 intermediate written to and read back from DRAM.\n\nAffected checkpoints include `nvidia/Qwen3.8-27B-NVFP4` and `RadixArk/Qwen3.8-27B-NVFP4`, both `MIXED_PRECISION` with 208 FP8 layers and 193 NVFP4 layers:\n\n```\nconfig.json -> quantization_config\n  quant_algo: MIXED_PRECISION\n  group_0: W8 A8   (208 targets, attention projections)\n  group_1: W4 A4   (193 targets, MLP + lm_head)\n```\n\nPassing `--quantization modelopt_fp4` explicitly does not help: `override_quantization_method` overwrites `self.quantization` and breaks out, so the resolved value is `modelopt_mixed` regardless.\n\n## Fix\n\nWhen the resolved method is `modelopt_mixed`, decide from the per-layer algorithms. `W4A16_NVFP4` is excluded deliberately: it quantizes weights only, so there is no activation quantization for the fusion passes to act on.\n\n## Not a duplicate of #55367\n\n#55367 fixes the same function for **compressed-tensors** mixed-precision checkpoints, where the top-level `format` is `mixed-precision` and NVFP4 has to be read out of `config_groups`. This change fixes the **ModelOpt** branch, where `quant_algo` is `MIXED_PRECISION` and the resolved method is `modelopt_mixed`. That PR's diff does not reference modelopt at all, so the ModelOpt path stays broken after it merges, and this change leaves the compressed-tensors path untouched.\n\nThe two touch the same function and will conflict textually. Either order works; whichever lands second needs a trivial rebase. Happy to fold this into #55367 instead if maintainers prefer a single change.\n\n## Test\n\n```\npre-commit run --files vllm/config/model.py tests/config/test_modelopt_mixed_nvfp4.py\n```\n\nAll hooks pass, including `ruff check`, `ruff format` and `mypy-3.10`.\n\nNew unit test `tests/config/test_modelopt_mixed_nvfp4.py` covers NVFP4 mixed with another algorithm, a mixed checkpoint with no NVFP4 layer, weight-only `W4A16_NVFP4`, and absent or malformed configs. A separate filename is used so it does not collide with the test file added by #55367.\n\nThe predicate was also run against the published `nvidia/Qwen3.8-27B-NVFP4` `config.json`, which returns True where it previously returned False.\n\n## Measured effect\n\nDGX Spark (GB10, sm121), `RadixArk/Qwen3.8-27B-NVFP4`, `vllm/vllm-openai:v0.28.0`.\n\nKernel level, median of 50 CUDA-event timings after 10 warmups, `intermediate_size=17408`, 64 layers:\n\n| M | unfused pair | fused | per forward pass |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| 2048 | 1.413 ms/layer | 0.755 | 90.4 -> 48.3 ms |\n| 8192 | 5.437 ms/layer | 2.920 | 347.9 -> 186.9 ms |\n\nEnd to end, TTFT at BS1 with `max_tokens=1` and randomised `prompt_token_ids` so prefix caching cannot serve them, median of 15, four arms in one server lifecycle each on one node:\n\n| vs no fusion | M=1024 | M=2048 | M=4096 | M=8192 |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| `fuse_act_quant` | -6.7% | -30.8% | -4.4% | -6.7% |\n| `fuse_norm_quant` | -1.9% | -26.8% | +0.0% | -1.7% |\n| both | -4.6% | -29.6% | -4.0% | -5.9% |\n\nNo arm is slower than baseline at any prefill length. The M=2048 column overstates: the unfused baseline is bimodal at that shape across server restarts (859.8 ms and 1172.1 ms for identical config in two runs), while M=8192 reproduced within 2%. The other columns are the trustworthy ones.\n\nDecode was checked separately at 32K cached prefix + 2K ISL + 256 OSL with MTP-3, concurrency 1/4/8, both arms as cells of one sweep on one node, prefix cache hit rate 85.3% in both:\n\n| concurrency | ITL | output throughput |\n|---|---|---|\n| 1 | 38.06 -> 37.99 ms | 20.03 -> 20.00 tok/s |\n| 4 | 53.44 -> 52.74 ms | 46.39 -> 46.40 tok/s |\n| 8 | 82.51 -> 81.46 ms | 60.27 -> 61.50 tok/s |\n\nInter-token latency is flat to slightly better at every concurrency, so enabling the fusion does not cost decode.\n\n## Accuracy\n\nThis change does not introduce or modify a kernel. It only lets an existing, already-default fusion pass apply to a checkpoint class it was being skipped for, and the same pass and fused op already run by default for non-mixed NVFP4 checkpoints. Greedy decoding was smoke-tested on both arms and produced identical output. Goal\n\nTPSP decomposes AllReduce into ReduceScatter (RS) + AllGather (AG), fusing RS/AG with adjacent compute (Norm, routing, GEMM) to hide communication behind computation. This brings roughly **10% TTFT improvement on long prefills**, which is especially valuable for today's agentic workloads where long-context prefill is common.\n\n\n## 1. TPSP Design Principles\n\n- **Conditionally enable TPSP based on measured performance:** A model may be compatible with TPSP without benefiting from it. Auto-profiling should identify the workload-specific crossover point, disable TPSP below it where overhead outweighs the gains, and enable it above it.\n- **Enable TPSP model by model:** TPSP changes model execution structure, so models should opt in individually until the approach is sufficiently established. Models not enabled for TPSP should continue using the existing path, with no TPSP-induced change in behavior or performance.\n- **Require device-side kernel support:** TPSP relies on fused kernels that may be model- and device-specific. If the required kernel is unavailable on a device, TPSP should automatically be disabled and the existing path used.\n- **Share the auto-profiling framework while allowing platform-specific tuning:** Provide common profiling logic that platforms can override or extend to account for their hardware characteristics. The profiling process should complete in about 60 seconds.\n\n---\n\n## 2. Dense Architecture\n\nTwo fusion sites per decoder layer: **o_proj** (after attention) and **down_proj** (after MLP).\n\n**Site A — o_proj → RS → Norm → AG**\n```\n            ┌────────┐   ┌──────────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌───────────┐\nhidden ───► │ o_proj │──►│ micro-chunk   │──►│  Norm   │──►│ AllGather │──► hidden [full,H]\n[full,H]    │ (GEMM) │   │ ReduceScatter │   │(+resid) │   │           │     → gate/up_proj\n            └────────┘   └──────────────┘   └─────────┘   └───────────┘\n```\nPer micro-chunk: `GEMM → partial`, `RS(partial) → reduced`, `Norm(reduced+residual)`, `AG(norm) → full hidden`.\n\n**Site B — down_proj → RS → (next layer's) Norm → AG**\n```\n            ┌───────────┐   ┌──────────────┐   ┌─────────────────────┐   ┌───────────┐\nhidden ───► │ down_proj │──►│ micro-chunk   │──►│ Norm (Layer L+1!)    │──►│ AllGather │──► hidden [full,H]\n[full,H]    │ (GEMM)    │   │ ReduceScatter │   │ pulled forward early │   │           │     → Layer L+1 attn\n            └───────────┘   └──────────────┘   └─────────────────────┘   └───────────┘\n```\nLayer L+1's pre-attention norm is pulled into Layer L's RS output, so AG feeds directly into the next layer with no standalone norm in between.\n\n**Notes:** RS accumulation uses peer-visible buffers with a generation-tagged completion signal; Norm fuses \"sum partials + residual + LayerNorm/RMSNorm\" in one pass; chunk `m+1`'s GEMM overlaps with chunk `m`'s RS/Norm/AG. AG's physical delivery mechanism is an implementation detail, out of scope here.\n\n---\n\n## 3. MoE Architecture (TP MoE only)\n\nSame two fusion sites as dense, but site ① interleaves with **routing**, and site ② interleaves with **expert permutation**.\n\n**Site ① — o_proj → RS → Norm → Top-K/Softmax → AG + Expert Permute**\n```\n            ┌────────┐   ┌──────┐   ┌────────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌───────────────┐\nhidden ───► │ o_proj │──►│  RS  │──►│Norm(+resid) │──►│ router  │──►│ top-k/softmax  │\n[full,H]    │ (GEMM) │   │      │   │  [shard,H]  │   │ logits  │   │  (per shard)   │\n            └────────┘   └──────┘   └────────────┘   └─────────┘   └───────┬───────┘\n                                          │                                  │ (expert_id, weight)\n                                          ▼                                  │\n                                    ┌───────────┐                            │\n                                    │ AllGather │◄───────────────────────────┘ (overlaps)\n                                    │hidden_norm │\n                                    └─────┬─────┘\n                                          ▼\n                                 ┌─────────────────┐\n                                 │ Expert Permute    │──► grouped_hidden ──► Group GEMM\n                                 └─────────────────┘\n```\nRouting is computed at shard granularity **before** AG, so permutation index work overlaps with the AG transfer instead of blocking on it.\n\n**Site ② — Group GEMM → (Expert Unpermute + RS) → (next layer's) Norm → AG**\n```\n            ┌────────────┐   ┌─────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────┐   ┌───────────┐\ngrouped   ─►│ Group GEMM │──►│ Expert Unpermute + RS     │──►│ residual + Norm(L+1)  │──►│ AllGather │──► hidden [full,H]\nhidden      │(per-expert │   │ (weighted sum to token   │   │   pulled forward      │   │           │     → Layer L+1\n            │    FFN)    │   │ order, fused w/ cross-   │   │                       │   │           │\n            │            │   │ rank reduction)          │   │                       │   │           │\n            └────────────┘   └─────────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────┘   └───────────┘\n```\nUnpermute and RS are **fused into one step**, not sequential: as each expert's output is scattered back to token order, it is simultaneously accumulated across ranks.\n\n**Dense vs. MoE:**\n\n| | Dense | MoE |\n|---|---|---|\n| Fusion sites | 2 | 2 (more complex) |\n| Pre-AG work | none | top-k/softmax at shard granularity |\n| GEMM type | standard | Group GEMM (variable per-expert batch) |\n| Reordering | none | permute + unpermute (fused with RS), overlapped with comm |\n| Load balancing | n/a | uneven expert load needs padding/capacity policy |\n\n**Notes:** Group GEMM needs variable per-expert batch support; routing math stays high-precision regardless of main dtype; both sites reuse the same RS/AG signaling as dense.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Implementation Plan\n\n| Stage | Goal |\n|---|---|\n| 1 https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60571 or https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/62385 | BF16 Dense |\n| 2 | BF16 MoE |\n| 3 | FP8/FP4 Dense |\n| 4 | FP8/FP4 MoE |\n| 5 | TPSP as default modeling |\n\n- **Stage 1/2** implement the Section 1/2 architectures as-is, on dense/MoE models respectively.\n- **Stage 3** inserts Quantize just before AG on a quantized dense model, keeping RS in higher precision: `GEMM → RS (high prec) → Norm → Quantize (fp8/fp4+scale) → AG (low-bit) → next GEMM`.\n- **Stage 4** layers Stage 3's quantization point onto the Stage 2 MoE pipeline using a quantized MoE model; routing stays unquantized.\n- **Stage 5** makes fusion the default: unified op registration, automatic graph rewriting of `proj → AllReduce → Norm` patterns, fallback to standard AllReduce when constraints/thresholds aren't met, numerical parity tests, broader model coverage, and performance benchmarking.\n\n### Proposed Change.\n\n- Model Integration\n- C++/CUDA operators and kernels\n- Auto-profiling and tuning\n- Platform backends\n\n### Before submitting a new issue...\n\n- [x] Make s", "created_at": "2026-10-08T06:29:35Z", "updated_at": "2026-10-19T04:58:18Z", "fetched_at": "2026-10-19T21:00:30Z", "state": "open", "merged_at": null, "parent": null, "tags": ["moe", "ttft", "performance", "communication", "nvfp4"], "metadata": {"number": 60559, "labels": [{"id": 6478559908, "node_id": "LA_kwDOI7xefs8AAAABgib6pA", "url": "https://api.github.com/repos/vllm-project/vllm/labels/RFC", "name": "RFC", "color": "DE9220", "default": false, "description": "", "archived_at": null, "archived_by": null}, {"id": 9988761457, "node_id": "LA_kwDOI7xefs8AAAACU2BncQ", "url": "https://api.github.com/repos/vllm-project/vllm/labels/quantization", "name": "quantization", "color": "62bfab", "default": false, "description": "", "archived_at": null, "archived_by": null}], "comments": 3, "state_reason": null}, "content_hash": "ecf909c36960a4a657a2a7a1033f32c66c0f623439955fbf5b25d162e63f657d", "rank": -22.552641591699835, "excerpt_truncated": true}, {"id": "github:vllm-project/vllm:59042", "source": "github", "kind": "pr", "url": "https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59042", "title": "[Bugfix][MoE] Apply expert_map remapping for CUTLASS FP4 MOE expert parallel", "body": "## Purpose\r\nFixes silent MoE corruption when running CUTLASS NVFP4 or MXFP4 MoE with expert parallelism.\r\n\r\nCutlassExpertsFp4 and CutlassExpertsMxfp4 accepted expert_map in apply() but never used it. The ordered precision policy should be configurable. Examples include:\n\n```text\nBF16 -> FP8\nBF16 -> FP8 -> NVFP4\nBF16 -> NVFP4\nFP8 -> NVFP4\nNVFP4 -> a future lower-precision format\n```\n\nExample percentage-based policies:\n\n```text\nnewest 20%: BF16\nnext 20%: FP8 E4M3\noldest 60%: NVFP4\n```\n\nor, for a cache whose highest selected precision is FP8:\n\n```text\nnewest 20%: FP8 E4M3\noldest 80%: NVFP4\n```\n\nNew KV entries and the active partially filled tail block would be written into the **first/highest tier selected by the policy**, which may be BF16, FP8, NVFP4, or another supported format. Once a block is complete and crosses a configured age boundary, it could be asynchronously quantized and moved to the next lower-precision pool.\n\nThis proposal is specifically about using different KV-cache formats **within the same attention layer based on block age**. It is different from layer-wise mixed precision, where different transformer layers use different formats.\n\n## Motivation\n\nLong-context KV caches consume a substantial amount of GPU memory. A uniform low-precision KV cache reduces memory usage, but it applies the same precision to recent and old entries.\n\nFor long-context and agentic workloads, it may be useful to preserve a recent window in the policy's selected highest precision while compressing older context more aggressively.\n\nThe intended abstraction is not a single special-case mode such as BF16 + FP8 + NVFP4. It is a generic ordered list of storage tiers. For example:\n\n- a BF16-based policy can progressively demote blocks into FP8 and NVFP4;\n- an FP8-based policy can progressively demote blocks into NVFP4;\n- an NVFP4-based policy could use a future lower-precision storage format when one is supported;\n- different hardware backends may expose different valid tier combinations.\n\nThis should be treated as a hypothesis that needs quality and performance benchmarking rather than an assumption that recent tokens are always more important.\n\n## Example memory calculation\n\nThe following example uses a Llama-3.1-70B-style GQA configuration:\n\n- context length: 64,000 tokens\n- number of layers: 80\n- KV heads: 8\n- head dimension: 128\n- both K and V are stored\n- one fully populated sequence\n- values are aggregated across all tensor-parallel ranks\n\nThe number of stored scalar values is:\n\n```text\n64,000 tokens × 80 layers × 2 (K and V) × 8 KV heads × 128 dimensions\n= 10,485,760,000 values\n```\n\nThe generic calculation for a progressive policy is:\n\n```text\neffective bits/value = sum(tier_fraction × tier_effective_bits)\n\nKV-cache size =\n    number_of_values × effective_bits_per_value / 8\n```\n\nFor NVFP4, the estimates below assume packed 4-bit data and one FP8 block scale per 16 values, or approximately 4.5 effective bits per value. FP8 is approximated as 8 bits per value. Small allocator, alignment, block-table, tensor-scale, and kernel-workspace overheads are not included.\n\n### Absolute VRAM comparison\n\n| KV-cache policy | Effective bits/value | Total KV cache | Per GPU with TP=8 | Saving vs uniform BF16 | Capacity vs uniform BF16 |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| Uniform BF16 | 16.0 | 19.53 GiB | 2.44 GiB | 0.00% | 1.00x |\n| Progressive: 20% BF16 + 80% FP8 | 9.6 | 11.72 GiB | 1.46 GiB | 40.00% | 1.67x |\n| Uniform FP8 | 8.0 | 9.77 GiB | 1.22 GiB | 50.00% | 2.00x |\n| Progressive: 20% BF16 + 20% FP8 + 60% NVFP4 | 7.5 | 9.16 GiB | 1.14 GiB | 53.13% | 2.13x |\n| Progressive: 20% BF16 + 80% NVFP4 | 6.8 | 8.30 GiB | 1.04 GiB | 57.50% | 2.35x |\n| Progressive: 20% FP8 + 80% NVFP4 | 5.2 | 6.35 GiB | 0.79 GiB | 67.50% | 3.08x |\n| Uniform NVFP4 | ~4.5 | 5.49 GiB | 0.69 GiB | 71.88% | 3.56x |\n\n### Correct baseline for each progressive policy\n\nA progressive policy should be compared with a uniform cache using the policy's own highest selected precision.\n\n| Progressive policy | Uniform reference | Progressive KV size | Uniform reference size | Saving vs its own reference | Capacity vs its own reference |\n|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| 20% BF16 + 80% FP8 | Uniform BF16 | 11.72 GiB | 19.53 GiB | 40.00% | 1.67x |\n| 20% BF16 + 20% FP8 + 60% NVFP4 | Uniform BF16 | 9.16 GiB | 19.53 GiB | 53.13% | 2.13x |\n| 20% BF16 + 80% NVFP4 | Uniform BF16 | 8.30 GiB | 19.53 GiB | 57.50% | 2.35x |\n| 20% FP8 + 80% NVFP4 | Uniform FP8 | 6.35 GiB | 9.77 GiB | 35.00% | 1.54x |\n\nThis distinction is important:\n\n- `BF16 -> FP8 -> NVFP4` is a BF16-based progressive policy and should primarily be compared with uniform BF16.\n- `FP8 -> NVFP4` is an FP8-based progressive policy and should primarily be compared with uniform FP8.\n- Uniform FP8 is therefore not merely a competing alternative to one BF16-based progressive mode; it can itself be the highest tier of another progressive policy.\n- Uniform NVFP4 is the lowest uniform format in this example. The same abstraction should extend below NVFP4 when a supported lower-precision format becomes available.\n\n### Breakdown: BF16 -> FP8 -> NVFP4\n\nPolicy:\n\n```text\nnewest 20%: BF16\nnext 20%: FP8\noldest 60%: NVFP4\n```\n\n| Tier | Tokens | Format | Estimated memory |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| Recent/hot | 12,800 | BF16 | 3.91 GiB |\n| Intermediate/warm | 12,800 | FP8 | 1.95 GiB |\n| Old/cold | 38,400 | NVFP4 | 3.30 GiB |\n| **Total** | **64,000** | Mixed | **9.16 GiB** |\n\nCompared with uniform BF16, this policy saves approximately **10.38 GiB**, or **53.13%**, per 64K sequence.\n\n### Breakdown: FP8 -> NVFP4\n\nPolicy:\n\n```text\nnewest 20%: FP8\noldest 80%: NVFP4\n```\n\n| Tier | Tokens | Format | Estimated memory |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| Recent/hot | 12,800 | FP8 | 1.95 GiB |\n| Old/cold | 51,200 | NVFP4 | 4.39 GiB |\n| **Total** | **64,000** | Mixed | **6.35 GiB** |\n\nCompared with uniform FP8, this policy saves approximately **3.42 GiB**, or **35.00%**, per 64K sequence.\n\nThis is the appropriate FP8-level comparison. It demonstrates that recency-based progression can be applied even when the selected hot tier is already quantized.\n\n## Possible user-facing configuration\n\nThe configuration should accept an arbitrary ordered list of supported tiers rather than enabling one hardcoded BF16-based layout.\n\nPercentage-based policies:\n\n```bash\n--kv-cache-dtype-policy \"bf16:20%,fp8_e4m3:20%,nvfp4:60%\"\n```\n\n```bash\n--kv-cache-dtype-policy \"fp8_e4m3:20%,nvfp4:80%\"\n```\n\n```bash\n--kv-cache-dtype-policy \"bf16:20%,nvfp4:80%\"\n```\n\nAn absolute-window policy may be easier to provision in production:\n\n```bash\n--kv-cache-dtype-policy \\\n  \"bf16:last=4096,fp8_e4m3:last=12288,nvfp4:rest\"\n```\n\nor:\n\n```bash\n--kv-cache-dtype-policy \\\n  \"fp8_e4m3:last=4096,nvfp4:rest\"\n```\n\nThe exact CLI and configuration for", "created_at": "2026-07-20T12:30:05Z", "updated_at": "2026-10-19T01:47:04Z", "fetched_at": "2026-10-19T21:00:39Z", lines, mirroring the branches immediately around it.\n\n**Why per-channel matters rather than just using `FP8_PB_WO`:** blockwise FP8 requires both\ndimensions divisible by 128. Shapes that fail that constraint have no working FP8 path at all\ntoday. Concretely, on Qwen3.8-Flash-Next the hyper-connection projections are\n`(320, 10240)`, `(10240, 320)` and `(4, 10240)` — `out % 128` is 64 or 4, `in % 128` is 64 —\nso every one falls off the fast CUTLASS blockwise kernel. "github:vllm-project/vllm:57445", "source": "github", "kind": "issue", "url": "https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/57445", "title": "[RFC]: Layer-Wise Mixed-Precision KV Cache", "body": "Status: Draft for discussion. Scope: a four-part vLLM implementation stack (logical dtype, packed allocation, ModelOpt integration, and CPU offloading) plus a separate proposed manager-block optimization. Cache calculations below evaluate the full design including that optimization; none of the four PRs enables it.\n\n## Summary\n\nAllow a model to use different KV-cache formats in different attention layers, initially full FP8 K/V and full NVFP4 K/V. Preserve each layer's logical encoding through dispatch and allocation, and support both attention-only models and hybrid attention/Mamba models using existing attention kernels and the heterogeneous cache-packing infrastructure already used by DeepSeek V4.\n\nThis is **layer-wise** mixed precision. FP8 K with NVFP4 V inside the same layer is a separate feature and is not enabled by this RFC.\n\n## Motivation\n\nLayers can have different sensitivity to KV quantization. A checkpoint should be able to retain FP8 in selected layers and use NVFP4 elsewhere, without requiring a model-wide format or changes to attention kernels.\n\nTwo runtime assumptions make this difficult:\n\n- Physical tensor dtype alone does not fully describe the KV encoding or scales.\n- Legacy hybrid allocation couples attention block sizing to recurrent-state page sizes. Adding attention formats with different byte sizes can increase alignment requirements, padding, and prefix-cache block granularity.\n\nThe proposal separates logical encoding from physical page allocation. It enables quality/memory trade-off experiments; it does not prescribe the optimal layer recipe or claim an accuracy improvement for every model.\n\n## Proposed Change\n\n```text\nModelOpt checkpoint recipe       Explicit global KV dtype override\n             |                                  |\n             +----------> Effective per-layer dtype\n                                      |\n                          AttentionSpec.cache_dtype\n                              /                 \\\n                 Backend dtype dispatch     Exact cache-page specs\n                          |                         |\n                 Existing FP8/NVFP4 kernels  Packed block-pool allocation\n```\n\n### 1. Preserve Logical Dtype\n\nAdd `AttentionSpec.cache_dtype` and the generic `QuantizationConfig.get_kv_cache_dtype(prefix)` hook, defaulting to `None`. Preserve logical dtype and quantization mode during spec merges, and reject conflicting formats.\n\nFlashInfer uses an explicit global KV dtype first, then a concrete per-spec dtype. An ambiguous quantized spec fails clearly instead of guessing its encoding. Existing unquantized behavior and skip-layer precedence are retained.\n\n### 2. Reuse DeepSeek V4 Cache Packing\n\nDeepSeek V4 already needs caches with different page sizes, such as MLA, indexer, and sliding-window caches. vLLM's existing infrastructure represents those pages separately and packs compatible layers into cache groups. We reuse that mechanism for pages whose sizes differ because of FP8/NVFP4 encoding.\n\n| Existing mechanism | Reuse for layer-wise mixed precision |\n|---|---|\n| `UniformTypeKVCacheSpecs` | Retain each layer's exact spec and page size within a compatible group. |\n| `_get_packed_kv_cache_groups()` | Group layers by compatible cache behavior, without requiring identical page sizes. |\n| Block-outer allocation and per-layer offsets/strides | Place FP8 and NVFP4 pages side by side and expose each layer's view to its existing kernel. |\n| Shared block pool | Size rows for the widest packed group; a block ID belongs to one group at a time. |\n\nHere, \"uniform type\" means compatible token-slot requirements, not necessarily the same encoding or byte size. Attention and Mamba state still retain their distinct cache semantics; we do not merge them into one attention spec.\n\nThe packed-group builder and core allocator are reused without changes in this stack. The additions preserve per-layer dtype, enable a compatible layout, avoid premature page-size unification, and consume checkpoint recipes. This does **not** reuse DeepSeek's model-specific compression algorithm, attention kernels, or token-selection policy.\n\nAdd a default-disabled `has_layerwise_kv_cache()` capability. When supported, reuse `UniformTypeKVCacheSpecs` and block-outer allocation to pack exact per-layer attention and state-cache pages. Bypass legacy hybrid page-size unification only for compatible configurations; incompatible layouts retain the existing aligned-page fallback.\n\nThe shared pool row must fit the widest packed group. Exact per-layer sizes therefore remove per-layer padding, but **do not eliminate pool-row slack**. Wrapped Mamba specs must still initialize the Mamba SSU backend.\n\nThe current implementation keeps the manager block equal to the supported kernel page: P64 in the validated FlashInfer NVFP4 configuration. The full design additionally considers larger manager blocks containing multiple P64 kernel pages, as described below. These larger blocks require a separately validated subpage layout; they are not currently supported merely by setting a larger `--block-size`.\n\n### 3. Enable ModelOpt Recipes\n\nConsume schema-v1 `kv_cache_quantized_layers` metadata, resolve attention-layer prefixes, and map FP8 to `fp8_e4m3` and NVFP4 to `nvfp4`. Validate invalid or incomplete recipes and preserve legacy metadata handling.\n\nWith `--kv-cache-dtype auto`, use the checkpoint recipe. An explicit global dtype overrides it. Weight quantization remains independent of the KV recipe. Producer-side support is tracked in [Model-Optimizer #2272](https://github.com/NVIDIA/Model-Optimizer/pull/2272).\n\n### 4. Preserve Packed Layouts for CPU Offloading\n\nThe built-in `OffloadingConnector` normally requests `LBHNC`, which can bypass packed hybrid allocation and select padded pages that cannot be split into supported kernel pages. For configurations advertising `has_layerwise_kv_cache()`, let the allocator select the compatible layout instead. Ordinary non-layerwise configurations keep their existing layout requirement; HiSparse retains its explicit requirement.\n\nReuse the existing packed-row CPU transfer path to preserve quantized attention payloads, scales, and recurrent-state bytes without format conversion. This supports store/reload with the same model, recipe, and parallel topology. It still transfers the complete backing row, including unused space; it does not implement compact CPU slots, new transfer kernels, or manager-block optimization.\n\n### 5. Proposed Manager-Block Optimization (Separate Follow-Up)\n\nExact per-layer sizes alone do not resolve shared-pool slack. Size the manager block so several small attention pages fit the allocation size needed by a state group, while keeping attention kernel pages at P64. Attention and state groups still own separate pool blocks; this does not co-locate a live state and attention payload in the same row.\n\nThe design estimate evaluates manager-block multiples of 64 through 1,024 tokens, scores the complete requ", "created_at": "2026-09-17T21:39:48Z", "updated_at": "2026-09-29T18:27:22Z", "fetched_at": "2026-10-19T20:54:22Z", "state": "open", "merged_at": null, "parent": null, "tags": ["deepseek", "mla", "throughput", "performance", "speculation", "kv-cache", "nvfp4"], "metadata": {"number": 57445, "labels": [{"id": 9988761457, "node_id": "LA_kwDOI7xefs8AAAACU2BncQ", "url": "https://api.github.com/repos/vllm-project/vllm/labels/quantization", "name": "quantization", "color": "62bfab", "default": false, "description": "", "archived_at": null, "archived_by": null}], "comments": 0, "state_reason": null}, "content_hash": "9076b496045892893a399cfe3c44fc427589ff341bef057a588bd1528248ba94", "rank": -24.599967681260246, "excerpt_truncated": true}, {"id": "github:vllm-project/vllm:50416", "source": "github", "kind": "issue", "url": "https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/50416", "title": "[Performance]: FlashInfer NVFP4 KV cache causal prefill is ~1.7-1.8x slower than FP8 on SM120", "body": "### Summary\n\nOn NVIDIA consumer Blackwell (SM120), FlashInfer attention with an NVFP4 KV cache shows a significant causal prefill regression compared with an FP8 KV cache.\n\nIn a matched profiler segment with the same 48 causal paged-prefill kernel calls, the NVFP4 path took about **145.8 ms**, while the FP8 path took about **84.0 ms**. This makes NVFP4 prefill approximately **1.74x slower** (about 1.7-1.8x across measurements).\n\nThe profiler indicates that the current SM120 path uses BF16 Q with packed NVFP4 KV:\n\n```text\nNVFP4:\nBatchPrefillWithPagedKVCacheKernel<\n  ...,\n  __nv_bfloat16,      # Q\n  __nv_fp4x2_e2m1,    # KV\n  __nv_bfloat16,      # output\n  ...\n>\n\nFP8:\nBatchPrefillWithPagedKVCacheKernel<\n  ...,\n  __nv_bfloat16,      # Q\n  __nv_fp8_e4m3,      # KV\n  __nv_bfloat16,      # output\n  ...\n>\n```\n\nThe regression appears to come from the existing SM120 NVFP4 software unpack/dequant path: packed FP4 KV values and their FP8 block scales must be unpacked, converted, and scaled before the BF16 QK/PV computation. Prefill is compute-heavy, so the KV bandwidth reduction does not compensate for this per-element conversion overhead.\n\n### Environment\n\n```text\nGPU: NVIDIA RTX PRO 5000 Blackwell, compute capability 12.0 (SM120)\nPyTorch: 2.10.0+cu129\nCUDA: 12.9\nFlashInfer: 0.6.15\nAttention backend: FLASHINFER\nKV cache layout: HND\nModel dtype / Q dtype on this path: BF16\nKV cache modes compared: nvfp4 and fp8_e4m3\n```\n\nThe vLLM code includes the recently added NVFP4 KV cache path. The NVFP4 cache is represented as packed E2M1 data plus one FP8 block-scale value per 16 head-dimension elements.\n\n### Kernel-level profile\n\nA comparable trace segment was selected from each profile. Both segments contain exactly 48 causal prefill calls:\n\n| Metric | NVFP4 KV | FP8 KV | NVFP4 / FP8 |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| Causal paged-prefill calls | 48 | 48 | 1.00x |\n| Causal paged-prefill total | 145.81 ms | 84.03 ms | 1.74x |\n| Mean per call | 3.04 ms | 1.75 ms | 1.74x |\n| CUTLASS GEMM total in the same segment | 93.61 ms | 96.69 ms | 0.97x |\n| All GPU kernels in the segment | 297.73 ms | 238.45 ms | 1.25x |\n\nThe similar CUTLASS GEMM time suggests that the model's weight/MoE kernels are not the primary source of this difference. Most of the additional GPU time is inside `BatchPrefillWithPagedKVCacheKernel`.\n\n### Decode and KV append observations\n\nThe same profiles show that NVFP4 is beneficial for decode:\n\n| Kernel | NVFP4 KV | FP8 KV |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| Single-token/decode paged attention | ~9.63 us/call | ~12.35 us/call |\n| KV append/write | ~8.39 us/call | ~3.01 us/call |\n\nNVFP4 decode is about 22% faster in this workload because decode is more bandwidth-bound. The NVFP4 append kernel is about 2.8x slower because it performs quantization, packing, block-scale generation, and slot mapping, but its cumulative cost is only around 7 ms and is not the end-to-end bottleneck.\n\n### End-to-end capacity stress result\n\nWith 30 concurrent requests:\n\n| KV dtype | Completed | Preemptions | Peak running | Peak cache | Wall time |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| NVFP4 | 30/30 | 0 | 30 | 68.6% | 102.3 s |\n| FP8 | 30/30 | 6 | 24 | 97.6% | 65.4 s |\n\nNVFP4 provides the expected capacity benefit and avoids preemption, but the causal-prefill regression dominates this particular workload.\n\n### Expected behavior / proposal\n\nOn SM120, the packed NVFP4 KV representation matches the hardware block-scaled FP4 MMA layout well. An optimized path could:\n\n1. quantize Q to per-16-block NVFP4 on the fly;\n2. execute QK directly with SM120 block-scaled NVFP4 MMA;\n3. avoid expanding K through the FP4 -> FP16/BF16 conversion chain before QK;\n4. retain the existing dequantized P x V path where the V-scale axis does not match the MMA reduction axis.\n\nThis is similar to the A4Q approach demonstrated for SM120/SM121, where an opt-in FlashInfer kernel uses FP4 Q x FP4 K block-scaled MMA.\n\nWould the vLLM maintainers be interested in tracking or integrating an optimized FlashInfer A4Q-style path for NVFP4 KV prefill on SM120? At minimum, it would be useful to document that the current NVFP4 KV path trades substantial prefill performance for capacity/decode benefits on this architecture.\n\n### Additional notes\n\n- The NVFP4 cache itself is correctly compact: packed FP4 data plus FP8 scales uses about 56.25% of FP8 KV storage, giving an expected capacity ratio of about 1.78x.\n- The two profiler JSON files contain more than one non-contiguous capture interval, so the comparison above uses matched segments instead of comparing total trace file size or total event counts.\n- Full profiler JSON files can be provided if useful.\n- Related NVFP4 KV integration discussion: #49011 and #32220.\n\n### Before submitting a new issue...\n\n- [x] Searched existing and past issues for NVFP4/FP4 KV prefill performance and unpack/dequant regressions.\n", "created_at": "2026-07-30T09:14:33Z", "updated_at": "2026-10-17T16:35:19Z", "fetched_at": "2026-10-19T21:01:01Z", "state": "open", "merged_at": null,
